Economist. London. Nr.37, 11.Mai 1844. S.771:"...no country can consistently have more than one standard of the measure of value; for whatever that standard may be, it must be uniform and un- changing. No article has a uniform and unchanging relative value with any other article: it only has such with itself. One piece of gold is always of the same value as another piece, of exactly the same weight and fineness, in the same place; but this cannot be said of gold and any other article. A certain piece of gold that will purchase a certain piece of silver to-day, may purchase less or more silver to-morrow ..."

731.13—21 John Fullarton: On the regulation of currencies... 2.ed. London 1845. S. 102—104: "To create a demand ... for additional currency, a mere rise of prices ... does not seem to be of itself sufficient. To produce that effect, the rise must also be accompanied by an increase of production and consumption. A rise in the price of any given article, or class of articles, occasioned by a deficiency of the supply, whether arising from adverse seasons or any other usual cause of scarcity, must in general be compensated by the diminution of the quantity of the article to be distributed, so that the distribution may still be accomplished with the same amount of money ... Where, on the other hand, a rise of prices is not the effect of natural dearth,

but of an augmented demand for commodities, where a fresh stimu- lus has been given to industry by the opening of new foreign markets or the extension of old markets ... in such cases, an increased scale of production and consumption is frequently found for a time to be compatible with an advance in the price of many articles of com- merce, and the general sum of transactions requiring the inter- vention of money may thus be multiplied in number and enlarged

1mo. The prices ... of commodities, are always proportioned to the

2do. The coin and current money in a country, is the representation

is more or less of this representation, (money) there goes a greater

or less quantity of the represented (commodities, etc.) to the same

quantity of it...

3"°. Increase commodities, they become cheaper, increase money,

they rise in their value."

737.11-17 The currency theory reviewed ... Edinburgh 1845. S. 69/70.

„The currency theory" oder „the currency principle" — so nannte man eine im zweiten Viertel des 19. Jahrhunderts in England weit- verbreitete Geldtheorie, die von der Ricardoschen Quantitätstheorie des Geldes ausging. Die Vertreter des „currency principle" — Sa- muel Jones Loyd (seit 1850 Lord Overstone), Robert Torrens, George Warde Norman, William Clay, George Arbuthnot und andere — wollten die abstrakten Gesetze der Metallzirkulation auf die Bank- notenemission ausdehen. Zur „currency" rechneten sie außer dem Metallgeld auch die Banknoten. Sie glaubten, einen stabilen Geld- umlauf durch volle Golddeckung der Banknoten zu erreichen; die Emission sollte entsprechend dem Edelmetallimport und -export reguliert werden. Die Versuche der englischen Regierung, sich auf diese Theorie zu stützen (Bankgesetze von 1844 und 1845), hatten wenig Erfolg und bestätigten nur ihre wissenschaftliche Unhalt- barkeit und ihre Untauglichkeit für praktische Zwecke.

737.14 'pluggers'] Von „plugged money", d.h. angebohrtes und mit Blei und anderen unedlen Metallen wieder ausgefülltes Silbergeld.

737.15 'sweaters'] Von „to sweat coins", d. h. Goldstücke durch Schütteln (ζ. B. in einer Ledertasche) leichter machen.

737.25-28 Bei Morrison, 2. ed., London 1837, S. 62: "We have next to provide for the full value and integrity of the coinage. With respect to the gold currency, this is already done to our hand by the law which enacts that, if a gold sovereign be more than 0,774 grain deficient in weight, it shall no longer pass current: but no such law exists with regard to our silver money ..."

737.29 Currencyleute] D. h. die Anhänger der „currency theory". Siehe Erl. 737.11-17.

737.29—30 that bis quantity] Mit diesen Worten formuliert John Fullarton in seinem Buch „On the regulation of currencies ..." (2. ed., London 1845, S. 13) eine der Hauptthesen der Vertreter der „currency theory". — Hervorhebung von Marx.

738.16 dem es untergeht] Im Sinne des englischen „which it undergoes", d. h., dem es ausgesetzt ist, dessen es sich unterzieht.

738.28—739.39 Literature. [Rezension zu:] A sketch of the history of the currency, comprising a brief review of the opinions of the most eminent writers on the subject. By James Maclaren. London: Groombridge. 1858. In: The Economist. London. Nr. 768, 15. Mai 1858. S. 536/537. - Diese

744.24—26 Bei Dodd: "Mr.Cotton was led to the conception of his beautiful machine by observing the injustice which the Bank sometimes un- consciously inflicted on its customers. Sovereigns which were issued from one counter at the Bank as being of full weight, were refused at another counter as being light. The scales may not have been equally delicate, or the liability to error on the part of the weighers (the 'personal equation', as astronomers would term it) may not have been exactly equal. An expert weigher..."
